The official "Master Plan for the Development of Samaria and Judea to the year 2010" (1983) foresaw the creation of a belt of concentrated Jewish settlements linked to each other and Israel beyond the Green line while disrupting the same links joining Palestinian towns and villages along the north–south highway, impeding any parallel [ribbon development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribbon_development) for Arabs and leaving the West Bankers scattered, unable to build up larger metropolitan infrastructure, and out of sight of the Israeli settlements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELustick201833–34,_35-311] The result has been called a process of "[enclavization](4848945.md)",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFalah20051334-312] [ghettoization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghetto),[^cite_note-313] typified most visibly by the enclosure of [Qalqilya](851829.md) in a concrete wall,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFields20173-314] or what Ariel Sharon called the [Bantustan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantustan) model,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarsakh2005231-315] an allusion to the [apartheid system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid), and one which many argue, makes [Israel's occupational policies not dissimilar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_and_apartheid), despite different origins, from the South African model.[^cite_note-316] In particular it bears comparison to the policies applied in South Africa to the [Transkei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transkei),[^cite_note-317] a policy that may have a broader geopolitical reach, if the [Yinon Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yinon_Plan) is to be taken as an indication of Israeli policy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPieterse198471-318] The World Bank argued in 2009 that creating economic islands in the West Bank and Gaza is a developmental dead-end that would only imperil the construction of an economically unified and viable Palestinian state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank20096-319]

One observed function of the [Separation Barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_Barrier) is to seize large swathes of land thought important for future settlement projects, notoriously in the case of the area of [Susya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susya) absorbing land worked by Bedouin herders with proven Ottoman title to the terrain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShulman200757-320] The construction, significantly inspired by the ideas of [Arnon Soffer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnon_Soffer) to "preserve Israel as an Island of Westernization in a Crazy Region",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Chatel2011134-321] had as its public rationale the idea of defending Israel against terroristic attacks, but was designed at the same time to incorporate a large swathe of West Bank Territory, much of it private Palestinian land: 73% of the area marked for inclusion into Israel was arable, fertile, and rich in water, formerly constituting the "breadbasket of Palestine".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Chatel2011131-322]

Had the barrier been constructed along the Green Line with the same purpose it would have run 313 kilometres, instead of 790 kilometres, and have cost far less than the \$3.587 billion the extended wall is estimated to cost (2009).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESwirski201020–21-323] The disparity arises from the government's decision to rope in dozens of settlements west of the barrier.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordisLevi201716-161] That it remains unfinished is said to be due to pressure from settler lobbies opposed to a completion that would restrict the further expansion of settlements or cut them off from Israel, as with [Gush Etzion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gush_Etzion).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordisLevi201718-324] There are only 12 gates through the 168 kilometres of the wall surrounding East Jerusalem, of which in theory four allow access to West Bankers who manage to obtain a permit. A whole generation of West Bankers have never seen the city, or the [Haram al Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram_al_Sharif), a denial of international law stipulating the right of access to sites of worship.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakdisi201068–69-325]

### Legal system

Further information: [Israeli law in the West Bank settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_law_in_the_West_Bank_settlements)

The Israeli-Palestinian conflict is characterized by a legal asymmetry,[^cite_note-326] which embodies a fragmented jurisdiction throughout the West Bank,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhalilDel_Sarto2015129–154-327] where ethnicity determines what legal system one will be tried under.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2008143–144-328] According to [Michael Sfard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Sfard) and others, the intricate military system of laws imposed on Palestinians has enabled rather than limited violence.[^cite_note-330] Down to 1967, people in the West Bank lived under one unified system of laws applied by a single judicial system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEImseis2000473-331] State law (*[qanun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qanun_\(law\))*) is a relatively alien concept in Palestinian culture, where a combination of the [Shari'a](28840.md) and customary law (*[urf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urf)*) constitutes the normal frame of reference for relations within the basic social unity of the [family clan (*hamula*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clan).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly200627-332] Settlers are subject to Israeli civil law, Palestinians to the occupying arm's military law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakdisi2010143-333] Overall the Israeli system has been described as one where "Law, far from limiting the power of the state, is merely another way of exercising it."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBisharat201268-334] A Jewish settler can be detained up to 15 days, a Palestinian can be detained without charges being laid for 160 days.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakdisi2010142-335]

According to the legal framework of international law, a local population under occupation should continue to be bound by its own penal laws and tried in its own courts. However, under security provisions, local laws can be suspended by the occupying power and replaced with military orders enforced by military courts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnicef20136-336] In 1988, Israel amended its Security Code in such a way that international law could no longer be invoked before the military judges in their tribunals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBenvenisti2012216-337] The High Court upheld only one challenge to the more than 1,000 arbitrary military orders that had been imposed from 1967 down to 1990 and that are legally binding in the occupied territories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFinkelstein199148-338] Israeli businesses in the West Bank employing Palestinian labour drew up employment laws according to Jordanian law. This was ruled in 2007 by the Israeli Supreme Court to be discriminatory, and that Israeli law must apply in this area, but as of 2016, according to [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), the ruling has yet to be implemented, and the government states that it cannot enforce compliance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW2016-184]

### Freedom of movement

Main articles: [West Bank](33209.md), [Israeli checkpoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_checkpoint), and [Palestinian freedom of movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_freedom_of_movement)



Routine check of a Palestinian man by Israeli soldiers at checkpoint in [Hebron](38577.md)

The [Universal Declaration of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Declaration_of_Human_Rights) establishes [freedom of movement](1270497.md) as a fundamental human right.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJamjoum200258-339]

It has been said that for "Jewish settlers, roads *connect*; for Palestinians, they *separate*."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamrava201686-340] Between 1994 and 1997, the Israeli Defense Forces (IDF) built 180 miles of bypass roads in the territories, on appropriated land because they ran close to Palestinian villages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEfrat200685-341] The given aim was said to be to afford protection to settlers from Palestinian sniping, bombing, and drive-by shootings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGalchinsky2004117-173] [Permanent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_checkpoint) and [flying checkpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Random_checkpoint) (some 327 a month in 2017), barriers and restrictive networks restructure the West Bank into "land cells", freezing the flow of normal everyday Palestinian lives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHandel2010259,_261-342] [TAU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Aviv_University) emeritus professor Elisha Efrat argues they form an apartheid network of "octopus arms which hold a grip on Palestinian population centres."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEfrat200685-341] A large number of embankments, concrete slabs, manned checkpoints, mounds, trenches, iron gates, fences, and walls impede movement on primary and secondary roads. The result was to [cantonize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_\(country_subdivision\)) and fragment Palestinian townships, and cause endless obstacles to Palestinians going to work, schools, markets and relatives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHass20026-343][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHandel2008183–184-344] Women have died or had miscarriages while waiting for permission at a checkpoint to go to hospital.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBBC2008-345] The [World Bank](36752.md) that the impact of restrictions on movement for workers cost roughly US\$229 million per annum (2007) while additional costs from the circuitous routes people must drive totaled US\$185 million in 2013. In one village, [Kafr Qaddum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Qaddum), soldiers from the [Nahal Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahal_Brigade) planted explosive devices on a tract of land where demonstrators gather, as a "deterrence" measure: they were removed when a 7-year-old child was injured playing with one.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShezafKubovich2020-346]

In February 2022, Israel issued a 97-page ordinance for implementation by 5 July. The document relaces a former 4 page guideline regulating entry and exiting Palestinian areas. Strict limits are imposed on foreigners, students, businessmen, academics, and, in general, Palestinians with dual nationality visiting families there. The tightening of movements is expected to have major negative repercussions on the local Palestinian economy.[^cite_note-347]

### Village closures

Main article: [West Bank closures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Bank_closures)



Demonstration against road block, [Kafr Qaddum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Qaddum), March 2012

The closure ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) *seger*, [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language) *ighlaq*) policy operates on the basis of a pass system developed in 1991,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHass20026-343] and is divided into two types: a general closure restriction the movement of goods and people, except when a permit is given, from and to Israel and the West Bank and Gaza, developed in response to a series of stabbings in the former 1993, and the implementation of total closure over both areas. Aside from general closures, total closures were imposed for over 300 days from September 1993 after the [Declaration of Principles of the Oslo I Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_I_Accord) and late June 1996. The strictest total closure was put in place in the spring of 1996 in the wake of a [series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jaffa_Road_bus_bombings) of the [suicide bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dizengoff_Center_suicide_bombing) executed by the Gaza-Strip based organization of [Hamas](13913.md) in retaliation for the assassination of [Yahya Ayyash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_Ayyash), when the Israeli government imposed a total 2-week long ban on any movement by over 2 million Palestinians between 465 West Bank towns and villages, a measure repeated after the [deadly clashes arising from the archaeological excavations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Wall_Tunnel_riots) under the [Western Wall](75225.md) of the [Haram al Sharif/Temple Mount](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haram_al_Sharif).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiai2013129-348]

The IDF erected iron gates at the entrances to the overwhelming majority of Palestinian villages, allowing the army to shut them down at will, in minutes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEB'Tselem2017b-349] Notable examples of villages that have undergone long term isolation, with residents suffering extreme restrictions on movement, are [Nuaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuaman),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarecciaReynolds2006148-350] which was absorbed into the Jerusalem municipality while having its inhabitants classified as West Bankers,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakdisi201065-351] and [Kafr Qaddum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafr_Qaddum) which has had a permanent roadblock at its entrance for 14 years, from 2003, the same time the settlement of [Kedumim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kedumim) was established, and since 2011 its villagers have been protesting the roadblock, which requires them to travel a distance six times greater than the usual route to access Nablus.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMa'an2017-352]

Towards the end of the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) in [Kuwait](7515890.md), Israel again imposed a curfew on the West Bank (and Gaza) lasting seven weeks, causing devastating economic setbacks, with thousands of Palestinians fired from their jobs in Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiai2013134-353] Nablus was subject to total curfews for 200 days in two years (2002–2004).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakdisi2010187-354] During house raids, windows and doors were smashed, food stocks mashed up into an indistinct mush; grain stores, TVs, solar panels, water tanks and radios destroyed or impounded.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraff2015172-355]

It is routine for the Israeli authorities to impose a comprehensive closure over the West Bank during Jewish holidays[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiai2013129-348] like [Yom Kippur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur), [Pesach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pesach), [Sukkot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sukkot) and [Rosh Hashanah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosh_Hashanah), with an exception made for Jewish industrial areas in the territory. The reason given is to prevent terror attacks, and also to enable security personnel at checkpoints time off to enjoy these holidays.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGross2018-356] Such closures can at times last 11 days.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAhronheim2017-357]

### Marriage difficulties

Main article: [Palestinian right of return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_right_of_return)

Coming to terms with the problem of the [Palestinian right of return](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_right_of_return) while negotiating for UN recognition in 1948, Israel came up with a [family reunification programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_reunification), and was granted membership on the understanding that it would comply with international law in this regard.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEl-AhmedAbu-Zahra201624–39-358] The very word "return" (*awda*) was censored from being used in Palestinian newspapers as implying an existential threat to Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedman198398-256] In practice, Israel evaluates proposed family reunifications in terms of a perceived demographic or security threat. They were frozen in 2002. Families composed of a Jerusalemite spouse and a Palestinian from the West Bank (or Gaza) face enormous legal difficulties in attempts to live together, with most applications, subject to an intricate, on average decade-long, four-stage processing, rejected. Women with "foreign husbands" (those lacking a Palestinian identity card), are reportedly almost never allowed to rejoin their spouse. The 2003 [Citizenship and Entry into Israel Law (Temporary Provision), or CEIL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizenship_and_Entry_into_Israel_Law), subsequently renewed in 2016 imposed a ban on family unification between Israeli citizens or "permanent residents" and their spouses who are originally of the West Bank or Gaza. Such a provision does not apply, however, to Israeli settlers in the West Bank or (until 2005) Gaza. In such instances, the prohibition is explained in terms of "security concerns".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllabadiHardan201676–77-359][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKhalilDel_Sarto2015-360]

A Jerusalemite Palestinian who joins their spouse in the West Bank and thereby fails to maintain seven years of consecutive residence in East Jerusalem can have their residency right revoked.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllabadiHardan201671-361] According to B'Tselem, any of the over 2,000 Palestinians registered as absentee owners of property in the West Bank have been denied permission to re-enter for purposes like family reunifications because their return would compel the Israeli authorities to return their property, on which settlements have been established, to their original Palestinian owners.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHareuveni201112-362]

### Targeted assassinations

Main articles: [Israeli targeted killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_targeted_killings) and [List of Israeli assassinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Israeli_assassinations)

[Targeted assassinations](33928336.md) are acts of lethal selective violence undertaken against specific people identified as threats. Rumours emerged in the press around September 1989 that Israel had drawn up a wanted list, several of whom were subsequently killed, and it was speculated that the time Israel might be operating "[death squads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_squad)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStockton199087,_91–92-363] Israel first publicly acknowledged its use of the tactic against Hussein Abayat at [Beit Sahour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_Sahour) near [Bethlehem](4312.md) in November 2000. In its decision regarding the practice, the [Israeli Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Supreme_Court) in 2006 refrained from either endorsing or banning the tactic, but set forth four conditions – precaution, [military necessity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_necessity), follow-up investigation and [proportionality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportionality_\(law\))[^cite_note-364]- and stipulated that the legality must be adjudicated on a case-by-case analysis of the circumstances.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMelzer200832–33,_36-365] [Nils Melzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nils_Melzer) found the judgement to be a step forward but flawed in several key regards, particularly for failing to provide guidelines to determine when the practice would be permissible.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMelzer200834–36-366] According to one former official, cited by [Daniel Byman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Byman), on average Israel spends on average 10 hours planning a targeted killing operation and ten seconds on whether to proceed with the assassination or not.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByman2011319-367]

Of the 8,746 violent Palestinian deaths registered from 1987 to 2008, 836 were executed following the identification of individuals based on information gathered from collaborators.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBhavnaniMiodownikChoi2011a134-368] According to [B'Tselem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B'Tselem), an Israeli human rights organization, for the period between 2000 and the end of 2005, 114 civilians died as the result of [collateral damage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collateral_damage) as Israeli security forces successfully targeted 203 Palestinian militants.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByman200698-369] The figures from 9 November 2000 to 1 June 2007 indicate that Israeli assassinations killed 362 people, 237 being directly targeted and 149 bystanders collaterally.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMelzer2008405-370] One intelligence officer recounting the atmosphere in the operations room where assassinations were programmed and then witnessed on video, stated that worries about "collateral damage" never dampened the cheers greeting a successful targeting mission.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Guardian''_12_Sep2014-371]

### Surveillance



Cameras are ubiquitous in the old city, [East Jerusalem](343508.md)

Israel, in its capillary monitoring of Palestinians, has been called a [surveillance state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_surveillance) *par excellence*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETawil-Souri201558-372] The entire Palestinian population is kept under surveillance, regardless of intelligence concerns, using smartphones and [closed-circuit television](87179.md) (CCTV) cameras, some capable of seeing into homes, whose photos are then fed into the IDF's "Blue Wolf" tracking system, endowed with [facial recognition technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facial_recognition_system). This is a pared down version of *Wolf Pack*, a computer data base containing "profiles of virtually every Palestinian in the West Bank, including photographs of individuals, their family histories, education and a security rating for each person." The deployment of such systems is banned in Israel. IDF soldiers on West Bank checkpoint duty are not allowed to end their shifts until they have filled their quota of 50 photos of Palestinians passing the checkpoint, and details concerning them. Settlers have a parallel smartphone app, White Wolf, for scanning Palestinians. On top of this, military drones and balloons, as well as the invasive [Pegasus spyware](51424588.md) developed by [NSO Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSO_Group) for penetrating smartphones, form part of Israel's West Bank surveillance system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShalhoub-kevorkian201255–72-373][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZureik2001205–227-374][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKubovich2022-375][^cite_note-376]

[Among many Israeli critics of the occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_criticism_of_the_occupation), the activist [Jeff Halper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Halper) and the philosopher [Avishai Margalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avishai_Margalit) express concerns at the paralysing effect on Palestinians of intricate surveillance systems, of a "matrix of control" underlying the occupation. Soon after hostilities ceased, Israel began to count all items in households from televisions to refrigerators, stoves down to heads of livestock, orchards and tractors. Letters were checked and their addresses registered, and inventories were drawn up of workshops producing furniture, soap, textiles, sweets and even eating habits. While many innovations were introduced to improve workers' productivity, they can also be seen as control mechanisms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon20089-377] Forward military planners in Israel foresee the day when Israel will withdraw from parts of the West Bank: this will not end the Occupation, for thereafter they envisage an "invisible occupation"/"airborne occupation" or "occupation in disappearance" régime, with a continued capacity to control the physically evacuated territory with surveillance and strikes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeizman2012237–238-378]

One former Israeli intelligence officer involved in [Unit 8200](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_8200) likened the surveillance system to that in the German film *[The Lives of Others](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lives_of_Others)*, with the difference, in his view, that the Israeli monitoring was more efficient. While the Israeli public thinks, he stated, that this surveillance is focused on combating terrorism, in practice a significant amount of intelligence gathering targets innocent people with no record for militancy. No Palestinian was, he claimed, exempt from non-stop monitoring.[^cite_note-379] Any information enabling "extortion" or blackmail, such as evidence of marital infidelity, health problems requiring treatment in Israel or sexual orientation is regarded as relevant.[^cite_note-380] Israeli surveillance and strike presence over Palestinian areas is constant and intense, with former Shin Bet head [Avi Dichter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avi_Dichter) noting, "When a Palestinian child draws a picture of the sky, he doesn't draw it without a helicopter."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByman2006100-381]

### Technologies of control

[Ben Ehrenreich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Ehrenreich), citing [Gudrun Krämer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gudrun_Kr%C3%A4mer)'s description of the [British military suppression of the 1936 Palestinian Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936%E2%80%931939_Arab_revolt_in_Palestine), states that, aside from caning, all of the extreme measures adopted by the Mandatory authorities recur as standard practices in the way Israel manages the occupied territories.[^cite_note-382]

Scholars differ regarding how to classify the techniques of segregation and exclusion[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELentin20188-383] used to further Israeli control over the West Bank. For Jan Selby, there are five central planks to consolidate territorial colonization: (a) settlement construction; (b) land confiscation and engineering a bypass road network (c) drawing the local economy into dependence on Israel's larger one; (d) the creation of a dual legal system with different laws for Palestinians and Jewish settlers, with subsidies favouring the latter and (e) seeking local clients and patrons who would act according to Israel's bidding, and, in lieu of success in this regard, increased repression.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESelby2003b76-177]

Gershon Shafir has discerned a matrix of five technologies of Israeli domination over Palestinians (a) the permit system; (b) administrative detention; (c) deportation: (d) house demolitions, and (e) torture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShafir201735-384] [Richard Falk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Falk) adds [political assassinations](33928336.md), [extrajudicial punishments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_punishment) and the use of [collective punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_punishment) to the list.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFalk200223-385] According to [Neve Gordon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neve_Gordon), Israel uses [lawfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawfare) "to encode the field of human rights and in this way (has) help(ed) frame human rights work in Israel as a security threat."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2014318-386]

### Population transfer and deportations

&#x20;Israel was one of the High Contracting Parties to the Fourth Geneva Convention dealing specifically with protection of civilians in a war zone, and, as a signatory, underwrote Article 49 which reads:&#x20;

> Individual or mass forcible transfers, as well as deportations of protected persons from occupied territory to the territory of the Occupying Power or to that of any other country, occupied or not, are prohibited, regardless of their motive... The Occupying Power shall not deport or transfer parts of its own civilian population into the territory it occupies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETillman197875-387][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShahak1974184-388]

This final clause is absolute, allowing of no exceptions, and was ratified by Israel when it signed the Geneva Conventions on 6 July 1951.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGalchinsky2004117-173] The sentence was written to prevent the repetition of the practice of colonization established by certain powers, by which Germany was to be understood, of transferring their population to conquered territories for political and racial reasons in WW2.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGorenberg2007101-389] Furthermore, Article 76 of that convention excludes deportation as a punitive measure in stating that

> protected persons accused of offences shall be detained in the occupied country and, if convicted, they shall serve their sentences therein.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELesch1979101-390]

The principle is unambiguous – "an occupier cannot expel a single person, however much that person constitutes a security risk".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlayfair1988408-391]

According to one estimate, between 1967 and 1978 some 1,151 individuals were deported by Israel, including two whole tribes, dispatched into exile en masse from the area of the [Jordan Valley](12105764.md) in December 1967 and May 1969. To provide legal warrant for these measures, which contravene the Fourth Geneva Convention, Israel applied law 112 going back to the [British Mandatory government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine)'s [Defence (Emergency) Regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defence_\(Emergency\)_Regulations) which predated the Geneva Convention by 4 years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELesch1979101–131-392] These in turn went back to military emergency legislation devised to counteract the Palestinian war of opposition to British occupation and Jewish immigration in 1936–1939.[^cite_note-393] Fathers were most frequently affected in the early days: sundering families, the practice was arrest household heads at night in their homes and take them to a desert south of the Dead Sea where they were forced, at gunpoint or gunshot, to cross over into Jordan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShahak1974184-388]

For at least two years starting in mid-1970, Israel collected Palestinians from Israeli prisons and forced them across the Jordanian border in the [Negev Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negev_Desert).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkevot2021-394] Codenamed Operation Patient, this practice expelled at least 800 people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkevot2021-394] The archives on the operation remain mostly unavailable to researchers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkevot2021-394]

To this day, any Palestinian Jerusalemite can have his or her residency revoked by Israeli law if Jerusalem has not constituted, in the view of the Israeli authorities, their "centre of life" for seven consecutive years,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllabadiHardan201671-361] a revocation constituting a forced population transfer that has been applied to at least 14,595 Palestinians since 1967 (2016).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW2017b-395] The PLO, inspired by the precedent of the [SS Exodus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SS_Exodus), once endeavoured to [sail a "Ship of Return"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sol_Phryne) into [Haifa](55606.md) harbour with 135 Palestinians Israel had deported from the territories. [Mossad](27619007.md) assassinated with a car-bomb the three senior [Fatah](11104.md) officials organizing the event in [Limassol](57577.md), and then sunk the ship in the port.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergman2018312–313-396]

The forced transfer of Palestinians still takes place in the West Bank: in 2018 the Israeli Supreme Court gave the green light to expel the people of [Khan al-Ahmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khan_al-Ahmar_\(village\)) from their township to a rubbish dump outside [Abu Dis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Dis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELazaroff2018-114] Israel arrested at a checkpoint in February 2017 Maen Abu Hafez, a 23-year-old Palestinian, since he had no ID, and detained him under a deportation order in a prison for aliens in [Ramla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramla), Israel. He had been raised since the age of 3 in the [Jenin Refugee Camp](50997.md). Israel seeks to deport him to Brazil, though he speaks no Portuguese, his mother is Uruguayan and his Palestinian father deserted the family to return to Brazil in 1997 and has not been heard from since.[^cite_note-397]

### Censorship

Main article: [Censorship in the West Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_the_West_Bank)

In the West Bank both the British Mandatory "Defense Emergency Regulations of 1945, No. 88" – stipulating that "every article, picture, advertisement, decree and death notice must be submitted to military censors"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedman198399-178] –, and "Israeli Military Order No. IOI (1967)", amended by "Order No. 718 (1977)" and "No. 938 (1981)" concerning "the prohibition of incitement and adverse propaganda" formed the basis for censoring West Bank publications, poetry and literary productions. The civil and military censorship bureaus could overturn each other's decisions, making publishing permits increasingly difficult.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedman198397-398] No clear guidelines exist however, so even works translated from the Hebrew press, or theatrical productions permitted in Israel, such as [Hamlet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamlet), could be censored.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedman198398–99-399][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESlyomovics199127,_30–31-400] Criticism of settlements was disallowed,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedman198395-401] as were sentiments of national pride. Obituaries mourning the dead, or expressing pride in the fallen could be challenged.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedman198393–94-402] Even mentioning the word "Palestine" was forbidden.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaid199116-403] Under Israeli Military Order 101, Palestinians under military law were prohibited from demonstrating and publishing anything relating to a "political matter".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKane2016-404]

Newspapers could lose their licenses, without any reason given, on the basis of 1945 Emergency Regulation (Article 92/2).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedman198397-398] Travel permits to enable notable Palestinians like [Elias Freij](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elias_Freij), mayor of [Bethlehem](4312.md), to be interviewed abroad could be denied.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedman198397-398] Graffiti (*shi'arati*) protesting the occupation were prohibited unless approved by the military,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeteet1996146–147-405] and owners of walls were made responsible and fined for the graffiti, so the practice had to be banned by Palestinians since it became a large source of revenue for Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESlyomovics199118-254] Recently, surveillance of the internet, using software to ostensibly identify potential threats in social media posts led to the arrest of 800 Palestinians both by Israeli units and PA security forces, with 400 detained as "lone wolf terrorists" for what they wrote, though none had carried out attacks and, according to security expert, [Ronen Bergman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronen_Bergman), no algorithm could identify lone-wolf attackers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2017-406][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKane2016-404]

### Coercive collaboration

One of the first things Israel captured on conquering the West Bank was the archives of the [Jordanian Security Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Security_Directorate), whose information allowed them to turn informers in the territory for that service into informers for Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBhavnaniMiodownikChoi2011b75-407] Collaborators (*asafir*), broken in interrogation, and then planted in cells to persuade other prisoners to confess, began to be recruited in 1979.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHajjar2005198-408] The number of collaborators with Israel before the Oslo Accords was estimated at 30,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2010a105-409] According to [Haaretz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haaretz), [Shin Bet](311752.md) has used a number of "dirty" techniques to enlist Palestinians on its payroll as informers. These methods include exploiting people who have been identified as suffering from personal and economic hardships, people requesting family reunification, or a permit for medical treatment in Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEByman2006100-381]

### Taxation

Main article: [Taxation in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Palestine)

In international law no occupying power has the right to impose taxes in addition to those existing before the occupation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrace1990101-410] Under Military Order 31 of 27 June 1967, Israel took over the Jordanian taxation system, with a notable change: Israelis moving into settlements were exempt, being taxed under Israeli law,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaxendale1989134–135-411] while by 1988, the high income tax rate of 55% for people with incomes in the 8,000 [dinar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jordanian_dinar) bracket was squeezed so that it applied to those earning 5,231 JD. In Israel the 48% tax bracket applied to those who earned almost twice that amount.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaxendale1989135-412]

In 1988 the affluent entrepreneurial Christian town of [Beit Sahour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_Sahour), which had several hundred mainly family-run businesses, organized a tax boycott on the grounds they saw no benefits flow back from their taxes, basing their boycott on the principle of the [American colonial revolt against their British masters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Revolution), namely [no taxation without representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_taxation_without_representation).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKårtveit201493-413] They refused to pay [VAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VAT) and/or income taxes. 350 households of 1,000 were targeted and their bank accounts were frozen, while 500 more had their bank account confiscated or debited. Israel reacted with collective punishment, placing the town under a 42-day curfew. Residences were raided every day and business machinery, any equipment for commercial purposes, refrigerators, jewelry, money, household furniture and at times memorabilia, were confiscated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrace1990102-414] To shield soldiers from stone-throwing, cars were stopped and placed round the houses, while people were mustered to form [human shields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_shield). The value of the goods confiscated bore no relation to the taxes being imposed, and were auctioned off in Israel at an estimated 20% of their replacement value. The effect was to virtually wipe out Beit Sahour's productive base.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrace199099,_101–102-415]

## Collective punishment

Israel's use of [collective punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_punishment) measures, such as movement restrictions, shelling of residential areas, mass arrests, and the destruction of public health infrastructure[^cite_note-416] violates Articles 33 and 53 of the Fourth Geneva Convention.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJamjoum200258–65-417] Article 33 reads in part:

> No protected person may be punished for an offence he or she has not personally committed. Collective penalties and likewise all measures of intimidation or of terrorism are prohibited[^cite_note-418]

Collective punishment of Palestinians also can be traced back to British mandatory techniques in suppressing the 1936–1939 revolt[^cite_note-419] and has been reintroduced and in effect since the early days of the occupation, and was denounced by [Israel Shahak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Shahak) as early as 1974.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShahak1974181–186-420] Notoriety for the practice arose in 1988 when, in response to the killing of a suspected collaborator in the village, Israeli forces shut down [Qabatiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qabatiya), arrested 400 of the 7,000 inhabitants, bulldozed the homes of people suspected of involvement, cut all of its telephone lines, banned the importation of any form of food into the village or the export of stone from its quarries to Jordan, shutting off all contact with the outside world for almost 5 weeks (24 February – 3 April).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBregman2014152-421] In 2016 [Amnesty International](18947898.md) stated that the various measures taken in the [commercial and cultural heart of Hebron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Shuhada_Street) over 20 years of collective punishment have made life so difficult for Palestinians[^cite_note-422] that thousands of businesses and residents have been forcibly displaced, enabling Jewish settlers to take over more properties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAI20165–6-423]

### House demolitions

Main articles: [House demolition in the Israeli–Palestinian conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_demolition_in_the_Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_conflict) and [Moroccan Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moroccan_Quarter)



Israeli military forces arriving to demolish the Palestinian community of Khirbet Ein Karzaliyah on 8 January 2014, rendering homeless the entire population of 10 adults and 15 minors; the military returned a month later to demolish tents in which residents were living since the last demolition

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEB'Tselem2014-424]

[House demolition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_demolition) is considered a form of collective punishment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShahak1974184-388] According to the law of occupation, the destruction of property, save for reasons of absolute military necessity, is prohibited.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW2017a-190] The practice of demolishing Palestinian houses began within two days of the conquest of the area in the [Old City of Jerusalem](302972.md) known as the [Moroccan Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moroccan_Quarter), adjacent to the [Western Wall](75225.md). From the outset of the occupation of the Palestinian territories down to 2015, according to an estimate by the [ICAHD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Committee_Against_House_Demolitions), it has been estimated that Israel has razed 48,488 Palestinian structures, with a concomitant displacement of hundreds of thousands of Palestinians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEICAHD2017-425]

Israel regards its practice as directed against houses built without Israeli permits or a form of deterrence of terrorism, since a militant is thereby forced to consider the effect of his actions on his family. Between September 2000 and the end of 2004, of the 4,100 homes the IDF razed in the territories, 628, housing 3,983 people were undertaken as punishment because a member of a family had been involved in the Al Aqsa insurgency.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEfrat200689-426] From 2006 until 31 August 2018, Israel demolished at least 1,360 Palestinian residential units in the West Bank (not including East Jerusalem), causing 6,115 people – including at least 3,094 minors – to lose their homes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEB'Tselem2018a-427] 698 of these, homes to 2,948 Palestinians of whom 1,334 minors, were razed in the Jordan Valley (January 2006 – September 2017).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEB'Tselem2017c-181]

Even huts by shepherds, on which taxes have been duly paid, can be demolished.[^cite_note-428]

According to the UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA), between 1 January and 10 July 2026, attacks by Israeli settlers and the demolition of homes and other structures resulted in the displacement of more than 3,200 Palestinians across the West Bank. This equates to an average of 17 people displaced each day—double the daily average recorded over the preceding three years.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-a-430]

### Permit regime

Main article: [Israeli permit regime in the West Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_permit_regime_in_the_West_Bank)

From 1967, almost every aspect of ordinary everyday Palestinian life was subject to pervasive military regulations, calculated to number of 1,300 by 1996, from planting trees and importing books, to house extensions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeteet1996146-431] Military order 101 denied West Bankers the right to purchase any form of printed matter – books, posters, photographs and even paintings – from abroad (including from Israel) unless prior authorization had been obtained from the military.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShehadeh1985a159-432] In the first two decades Palestinians were required to apply for permits and licenses for an enormous number of things such as a driver's license, a telephone, trademark and birth registration, and a good conduct certificate to secure jobs in numerous professions. Obtaining such permits has been described as a *[via dolorosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Via_dolorosa)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBen-NaftaliSfardViterbo201852-433] The precise criteria to be satisfied for obtaining permits have never been clarified.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiai2013135-434] It has been likened to the pass system of Apartheid.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPieterse198465-435] [Zygmunt Bauman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zygmunt_Bauman)'s warnings of the debilitating effect bureaucracy may have on the human condition has been cited to throw light on the Orwellian or [Kafkaesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franz_Kafka) trap of red tape that, it is argued, places a stranglehold on Palestinian autonomy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZureik2015121-436] There are 42 types of permits, depending on the purpose of one's movements, required by Israeli authorities as of 2018.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEARIJ20187–8-437]

### Impact on education

Main article: [Education in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Palestine)

The high priority traditionally accorded education in Palestinian society continued over the early occupation, with, by 1979, Palestinians making up an estimated 10% of all Arab university graduates.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavies197965-45] Internal evidence from leaked reports in the 1960s suggest that improved high education for [Israeli Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_Arabs) was thought at the time to pose a potential a security threat.[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKattenburg2021-439] Israel signed the [International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Covenant_on_Economic,_Social_and_Cultural_Rights) in 1966, ratified in 1991. After 1967, Israel asserted that the right to education did not apply to the West Bank, East Jerusalem and Gaza, territories it held under occupation, since they lay outside Israel's sovereign borders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKattenburg2021-439]

During the first Intifada at one point Israel imposed a 19-month closure on all schools in the West Bank, including kindergartens, suggesting to at least one observer that Israel was intentionally aiming to disrupt the cognitive development of Palestinian youths.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraff2015173-440] In the first two years of the Al-Aqsa Intifada, 100 schools were fired on by the IDF, some were bombed and others occupied as military outposts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESait2004221-441] In 2017, according to one estimate, Israel had issued either demolition or "stop work" orders affecting 55 West Bank schools.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbdullah2017-442]

### Night raids

According to Major General [Tal Russo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tal_Russo), the IDF undertakes operations "all the time, every night, in all divisions."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarel2003-443] Israeli night raids are usually undertaken between 2 am and 4 am. The units, whose members are often masked and accompanied by dogs, arrive in full battle gear and secure entry by banging on doors or blowing them off their hinges. Surging blips in frequency may relate to rotation of new units into an area. Most occur in villages in close proximity to settlements. Such missions have several different purposes: to arrest suspects, conduct searches, map the internal structure of a dwelling, and photograph youths to improve recognition in future clashes. Laptops and cellphones are often seized, and, if returned, not infrequently damaged. Vandalism is commonplace, with looted objects given to needy soldiers or those on low pay, as in [Operation Defensive Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Defensive_Shield).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2010b146-444] Reports of stashes of money that go missing after a search are frequent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWCLAC20154–5-445] Many personal effects – photos of children or families, watches, medals, football trophies, books, Qur'ans, jewelry – are taken and stored away, and, according to one informant, intelligence officer trainees were allowed to take items of such Palestinian "memorabilia", called "booty", from storerooms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Guardian''_12_Sep2014-371] After international protests, in February 2014 a pilot scheme was begun to issue summonses instead of arresting children at night, and programmed to last until December 2015[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWCLAC20156-446] The purpose of mapping raids is, reportedly, to work out how an area looks from Palestinian angles for future planning to enable an option for "straw widows" operations (mounting ambushes from inside those homes).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHass2018a-447]

The practice by Israeli military units of raiding, making arrests in, and ransacking Palestinians homes deep in the night is a long-standing practice, persisting to the present day. In just three days over 21–23 January 2018, 41, 24 and 32 separate raids were made[^cite_note-448] In 2006 Israel made 6,666 raids inside the occupied territories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakdisi2010142-335] In the first six months of 2007, 3,144 Israeli search/arrest raids were made in the West Bank[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakdisi201063-449] the parents of 90% of minors arrested, blindfolded and handcuffed in night raids, were given no explanation for the abduction, nor information about where the child would be detained.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein20187-450] In another study, 72.7% of children studied had witnessed night raids, the traumatic experience coming second after watching scenes of mutilated or wounded bodies on TV.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThabatAbdullaElhelouVostanis2006130-451] An extrapolation from this figure would, according to the [NGO](46539.md) WCLAC, suggest that from the time martial law was imposed in June 1967 up to 2015, over 65,000 night raids had been conducted by the Israeli military on Palestinian homes in the West Bank (not including East Jerusalem).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWCLAC20152-452]

A 26-year-old Palestinian man named Abdul Rahman Usha was fatally shot during an Israeli operation at the [Fara refugee camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far'a_refugee_camp), located south of Tubas in the occupied West Bank, in November 2025, as reported by the official Wafa news agency.[^cite_note-453]

### Arrests and administrative detention

Main article: [Palestinian prisoners of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_prisoners_of_Israel)

An estimated 650,000 Palestinians were detained by Israel from 1967 to 2005,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakdisi2010142-335] one in three of all Palestinians in the first two decades alone.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPunamäki198882-454] The military court system, regarded as the institutional centerpiece of the occupation, treats Palestinians as "foreign civilians" and is presided over by Jewish Israeli judges[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHajjar20052,_5-455] drew on prior British Mandatory law, where its application to Jewish activists was vigorously protested by the [yishuv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yishuv) representatives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein197841–42-456] Four provisions entail (a) long detention of suspects incommunicado (b) without access to a lawyer (c) coercive interrogation to obtain evidence and (d) the use of "secret evidence".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHajjar20055-457] Over this period, tens of thousands have been subject to [administrative detention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_detention),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPelleg-Sryck2011123-458] whose rationale is to incarcerate suspects who, in conventional criminal law, might not be convictable.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen1985111-459] Taisir al-Arouri, a [Bir Zeit University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bir_Zeit_University) professor of mathematics, was arrested at night on 21 April 1974 and released on 18 January 1978, after suffering 45 months of imprisonment without trial or charges being laid, only after [Amnesty International](18947898.md) issued a public protest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAI1978339-460]

The practice was considered by one scholar in 1978 as "an aberration of criminal justice" of a provisory nature.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldstein197843-461] In 2017 [Amnesty International](18947898.md), noted that "hundreds of Palestinians, including children, civil society leaders and NGO workers were regularly under administrative detention",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAI2018b208–209-462] and regards some, such as [Khalida Jarrar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalida_Jarrar) and [Ahmad Qatamesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Qatamesh), as [prisoners of conscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoner_of_conscience).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAI2018b210-463][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAI2018a-464]

### Torture

Main article: [Israeli torture in the occupied territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_torture_in_the_occupied_territories)

States are obliged under the Fourth Geneva Convention to prevent torture, including mental suffering.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERon199485-465] According to [Lisa Hajjar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Hajjar) (2005) and Rachel Stroumsa, the director of the [Public Committee Against Torture in Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Committee_Against_Torture_in_Israel), torture has been an abiding characteristic of Israeli methods of interrogation of Palestinians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAharony2018-466][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHajjar2005195-467] Torture can be of two types, physical and psychological.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERon199486-468] Reports of torture emerged in the 1970s, and began to be documented in detail by the NGO [Al-Haq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Haq) in the mid-1980s. The 1987 the [Landau Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landau_Commission) examined some abuses and concluded "moderate physical pressure" was acceptable. The practice was then banned by Israel's High Court, barring case-by-case authorizations by the Attorney-General.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevinson2017-469]

The Hebrew army slang term *tertur* is associated with policies introduced by [Rafael Eitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafael_Eitan), who ordered army troops and [border police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Border_Police) to engage in repeated arrests and the humiliation of large numbers of the Palestinian population in the territories. This refers to practices such as the wholesale roundups that took place whenever West Bank Arabs staged nationalist demonstrations. Israeli border police have been witnessed forcing Arabs to sing the Israeli national anthem, slap each other's faces and crawl and bark like dogs. The police have also arrested thousands of Arabs each year on "security" charges, which have ranged from outright terrorism to simply reading blacklisted books.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAl-Haq19863–4-470]

### Children

Main article: [Children in the Israeli–Palestinian conflict](30873056.md)



Children detained by [Golani Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golani_Brigade) soldiers in [Hebron](38577.md)



Palestinian girls having their school bags searched by Israeli soldiers in [Tel Rumeida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tel_Rumeida), Hebron

Documented incidents of Israeli forces targeting and killing Palestinian children date back to the earliest days of the conflict.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1993181-471] Following the 1948 war, prior to Israel's formal occupation of the West Bank, border skirmishes between Israelis and Arabs were a regular occurrence with multiple cases involving Israeli soldiers shooting unarmed Palestinians, including children, dead.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris1993184-472] During the [1952 Beit Jala raid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Beit_Jala_raid), 4 children ranging in age from 6 to 14 were killed by machine gun fire.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutchison195612–16-473] During the First Intifada, Palestinian children regularly suffered serious, often fatal injuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHass2000-474] The First Intifada saw exceptionally high involvement by Palestinian teenagers, prompting Israel to declare stone throwing a felony under occupation law, a categorization that applied to children as well as adults and allowed for protracted incarceration of minors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrinkley1989-475] Ill-treatment of Palestinian children in the Israeli military detention system appears to be widespread, systematic and institutionalized.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein20185–26-476][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnicef201313-477] According to a 2013 study by the [United Nations International Children's Emergency Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_International_Children's_Emergency_Fund), covering 10 years of allegations of the mistreatment of children in Israel's military practices in the West Bank, though international law requires that all children in contact with judicial systems be treated with dignity and respect at all times, the evidence from multiple outside observers over a decade suggests Palestinian children under Israel military detention suffer cruel and degrading treatment. In law, the prohibition against such practices is "absolute and unconditional," and even security considerations or threats of war cannot override the rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnicef20132-478]

Children constitute half of the Palestinian population, and though often construed as "mute victims or misguided puppets", they actively engage in the resistance, with some arguing that in doing so they forfeit their rights.[^cite_note-479] According to James Graff, Palestinian children comprise a notable segment of Palestinians targeted, and can be included in categories from which they are normally exempted, and be singled out as a group to be subject to traumatizing violence, and targeted in random shootings, gassings and violence by soldiers and by settlers sponsored by the state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraff2015168–169-242]

According to the [Swedish branch of Save the Children](436885.md), between 23,600 and 29,900 children required medical treatment after suffering injuries from IDF beatings during the first two years of the First Intifada, a third of them aged 10 or under.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMearsheimerWalt20063–12-480] Under Yitzhak Rabin's 19 January 1988 order to employ "might, power and beatings" and an interview in which he spoke of the need to "break their bones",[^cite_note-481] beatings, which until then had usually been a hidden interrogation method, went public, until an outcry arose when journalists filmed the tactic, a scandal countered by issuing a ban on media entering the territories in the spring of 1988.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPeteet199436-482]

Minors (16 years old and under) adding up to 5% of the child population constituted 35–40% of the 130,000 Palestinians who suffered serious injuries from Israeli troops in this uprising.[^cite_note-483] Of 15-year-olds and under requiring medical treatment, 35% were injured by Israeli gunfire, 50% by beatings, and 14.5% suffered from tear gassing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraff2015157-484] From 2009 to 2018 Israel Security squads shot dead 93 Palestinian minors in West Bank clashes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEB'Tselem2018b-485] In the period of the Al-Aqsa uprising, the ratios of those killed indicate that roughly 20- 25% were children on both sides, with the difference that Israeli fatalities were from incidents of body-bombing in which they were not the primary targets, whereas a substantial proportion of Palestinian children were killed by Israeli sniper gun-fire directed individually, according to Frank Afflitto.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAfflitto200747-486] From September 2000-to December 2003, 109 children were killed by "one-shot wonders" in the head, 4 in the neck, and 56 by exclusive heart-chest shots. A further 90 were killed with two or three gunshot wounds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAfflitto200747–48-487] Overall, in the 3.25 years after the second uprising 427 children were shot dead by IDF forces and settlers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAfflitto200748-488]

## Resources

### Agriculture

Main article: [Agriculture in the Palestinian territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_the_Palestinian_territories)



Training of Israeli tanks, damaging the fields of [Ibziq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibziq) Palestinian village, December 2021

The pastoral economy was a fundamental wing of the Palestinian economy. Of the 2,180 square kilometres (840 mi<sup>2</sup>) of grazing land in the West Bank Israel permitted in the first years of the 21st century only 225 square kilometres (87 mi<sup>2</sup>) for such use.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERubenberg2003382-489] In certain areas, such as the South Hebron Hills, [Palestinian bedouin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_bedouin) shepherds have their grazing lands disseminated with poison pellets that kill off their flocks, and require minute gleaning and disposal to restore the land to health.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShulman200750–57-490] In Area C, nearly 500,000 dunams of arable land exist, Palestinian access to which is severely restricted while 137,000 are cultivated or occupied by Israeli settlements. Were the 326,400 dunams theoretically open to Palestinian use made available, the World Bank calculates, it would add \$US 1,068 billion to Palestinian productive capacities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiksicEddinCali201420-491] Another 1,000,000 dunams could be exploited for grazing or forestry, were Israel to lift its restrictions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiksicEddinCali201419ff-492] The World Bank estimates that were Palestinian agriculture given access to better water resources, they would benefit by a boost in agricultural output of around \$1 billion per annum.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJoyce2016-493]

After 1967, restrictions were placed on the types of fruit tree and vegetables that could be planted, and even the importation of tractors required an Israeli permit.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiai2013136-494] Just after the occupation, a trial study carried out on [Deir Dibwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deir_Dibwan)'s land, which is rich in underground water, concluded that it showed great promise as one of the best sites in the West Bank for growing oranges and bananas. An Israeli drilling permit could not be obtained, leading most of those involved in the project to emigrate to the U.S.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEscribanoEl-Joubeh1981152-495] Israel's control of land, water, trade and markets, and its specification and rigorous restrictions on what could be grown, is held responsible for the decline of agriculture as a share of West Bank GDP, and the drop of agricultural labourers in the work market from 46% to 27%, so that from 1993 to 1995 output declined by 40.12%. In the years directly preceding the [Al Aqsa uprising](71717.md) (1998–1999) the IDF and settlers uprooted 21,705 trees throughout the West Bank and Gaza Strip.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERubenberg2003123–124-496] From 2000 to 2009, [ARIJ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Applied_Research_Institute%E2%80%93Jerusalem) estimated that 1,628,126 fruit trees in the West Bank and Gaza were destroyed or uprooted by Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeneley2011278-497]

Destruction of agricultural goods was considerable during the second intifada. In the five months following its outbreak, 57,928 olive trees, 49,370 citrus trees, 22,270 stone-fruit trees, 11,514 date palms, 12,000 banana trees and 30,282 grapevines were uprooted. Olive oil production dropped 80% that year as a result.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERubenberg2003124-498] In the 15-month period from the outbreak, down to December 2001, the total damage was calculated as 155,343 olive trees, 150,356 citrus trees, 54,223 almond trees, 12,505 date palm trees, 39,227 grape vines, 18,400 banana trees, and 49,851 other varieties of tree.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERubenberg2003382-489] From September 2000 to December 2002, Israeli forces destroyed 14,196 forest trees. In the first two years, taking in the damage wrought by Israel in both the West Bank and the Gaza Strip, according to [Cheryl Rubenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheryl_Rubenberg), 667,000 trees were eradicated and 3,669,000 square metres of agricultural land destroyed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERubenberg2003124-498] According to Palestinian authorities, the restrictive allocation of water to Palestinian agriculture has remained constant, at 84 million cubic metres per annum, since 1967. The Oslo Accords foresaw a supplementary 70–80 million cubic metres being supplied, but in two decades only about half of this additional supply had been provided.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Chatel2011131-322]

The olive tree, aside from its economic function, is a symbol of Palestinian nationhood, of their quest for independence, much as the pine introduced by Zionist [arboriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arboriculture).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBraverman2009237–238-499] 15% of the territories – 45% of its arable land – is covered by olive groves, and is both a key resource, and its bimonthly autumnal harvesting a period of deep socioeconomic importance for families in most villages in the West Bank, for whom it provides roughly 40% (2009) of West Bank agricultural output. It has mopped up unemployment caused by job loss in Israel after the outbreak of the Second Intifada and the olive is known colloquially as *shajara el-fakir* (the pauper's tree), and regarded as holy (*shajara mubaraka*).[^cite_note-500] Their uprooting by state agencies or settlers is an everyday occurrence in the West Bank.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBraverman2009257-501]

Israeli officials see olive grove cultivation as "one of the best ownership techniques around".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBraverman2009242-253][^cite_note-502] One Israeli official likened Palestinian olive trees to Palestinian children. They look naïve but several years down the track they turn into [ticking bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticking_time_bomb_scenario).[^cite_note-503] The centrality of such olive groves for Palestinians is, according to [Michael Sfard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Sfard), viewed in the Zionist narrative as emblematic of "Arab laziness", since it grows alone and can be shaken down once a year to yield its wealth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBraverman2009243–244-504][^cite_note-505] In one analysis in 2006 it emerged that only 4% of complaints against settler trespass and destruction of Palestinian olive trees ever led to prosecution.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGordon2008143-506]

Following an [Ottoman](22278.md) practice of uprooting olive trees to punish tax evasion, Israel began destroying groves, but with the expressed purpose of increasing security for settlements, and visibility for its internal West Bank road system servicing the colonial infrastructure. Construction of the [Separation Barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Separation_Barrier), erected predominantly on West Bank land, led to the uprooting of tens of thousands of olive trees. In just one village, [Qafeen](851834.md), the wall's route led to the uprooting of 12,000 trees of this variety, while alienating the inhabitants from their groves with a further 100,000 trees left on the Israeli side in a seam-zone, which they may access only once a year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBraverman2009247-507][^cite_note-508] Aside from state practices, settlers have waged what one scholar terms "tree warfare" consisting in the stealing, uprooting, chopping or burning of native Palestinian olive groves, often as part of [price tag operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Price_tag_policy).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBraverman2009250–251-509] Of the 708,000 dunums of irrigable land in the West Bank only 247,000 dunums under aggregate irrigation, and it has been calculated (2009) the gross margin Palestinians forego touches close to \$480 million per annum, roughly about 10% of GDP. The collateral effect of loss for potential employment runs close to (upper estimate) 10,000 jobs. The World Bank has observed that only 35 per cent of irrigable Palestinian land is actually irrigated, which costs the economy 110,000 jobs and 10 per cent of GDP.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUNCTAD20167-510][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank200926–27-511]

### Water

Main articles: [Water supply and sanitation in Palestine](21378125.md) and [Israeli expropriation of Palestinian springs in the West Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_expropriation_of_Palestinian_springs_in_the_West_Bank)

In the wake of 1967, Israel abrogated Palestinian water rights in the West Bank,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank2009iv-512] and with Military Order 92 of August of that year invested all power over water management to the military authority,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBenvenisti2012215-513] Military order 158 of November of that year required Palestinians to obtain a permit from the military authorities before developing any new water installation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAI2017-514] As of 1996, no Palestinian had received a permit to drill a well since that date, by which time Israel drew a third of its fresh water and 50% of its drinking water, from the West Bank.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELonergan1996370–380-515] According to [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) Israel's confiscation of water violates the [Hague Regulations of 1907](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hague_Conventions_of_1899_and_1907), which prohibit an occupying power from expropriating the resources of occupied territory for its own benefit.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW2016-184]

Palestinians have complained that their economy and agriculture are badly affected by the depletion of village waters in favour of supplying settlements. Israel placed restrictive policies on West Bank users. Differentials in costs of supply to Palestinians and settlements, which consumed 8 to 10 times what Palestinians were allowed, was blatant: settlements paid 0.5 New Israeli shekels (NIS), while Palestinian villages paid 1.8 NIS, per m3, with the former supplied daily, while delivery to the latter was limited to one or two days a week.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELonergan1996380-516] "Blatant discrimination" exists in water pricing, allocation and delivery systems. Water consumption by Israeli settlers in the Territories is roughly eight to ten times that of Palestinians. Water is sold to Israeli settlements for 0.5 [New Israeli shekels (NIS)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_new_shekel) per m3, while it was sold to Palestinian villages for 1.8 NIS per m3.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELonergan1996380-516]

According to [John Cooley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_K._Cooley), West Bank Palestinian farmers' wells were a key element behind Israel's post-1967 strategy to keep the area and in order to protect "Jewish water supplies" from what was considered "encroachment"[^cite_note-517]

By 2013, though some villages had only 15 litres per person, it was estimated West Bank Palestinians were supplied an average per capita 70 litres per day, as opposed to the 280–300 litres per person for Jewish settlers. Sometimes the contrast is starker: [Al-Hadidiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Bikai'a)'s 20 litres per person versus the 431 litres per day consumed on the neighbouring Jewish [moshav](1597952.md) settlement of [Ro'i](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ro'i), which draws 431 litres per person per day from a well it drilled on Al-Hadidiya land.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldinger2013-518]

Israeli settlements have also pursued [a practice of taking over for their own uses numerous springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Israeli_expropriation_of_Palestinian_springs_in_the_West_Bank) belonging to Palestinian villages, appropriating them to create adjoining parks suitable to tourism. Palestinians are denied access.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOCHA2012-519][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELevyLevac2019-520]

### Waste zone

Further information: [Abu Dis Waste Disposal Site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Dis_Waste_Disposal_Site)

Israel ratified the international [Basel Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basel_Convention) treaty on Israel on 14 December 1994, according to which, any transfer of waste must be performed with an awareness of the dangers posed to the disempowered occupied people. It forbids the creation of "environmental [sacrifice zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrifice_zone)" in their midst.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAloni201716-521] Israel, it is argued, has used the West Bank as a "sacrifice" zone by placing 15 waste treatment plants there under less stringent rules than those required in the Israeli legal system, thereby exposing the local people and environment to hazardous materials. The military authorities do not render public the details of these operations. These materials consist of such things as sewage sludge, infectious medical waste, used oils, solvents, metals, electronic waste and batteries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAloni20175–6-522]

Within Israel strict environment laws apply. Of 121 settlements studied in 2007, 81 had wastewater facilities, many subject to breakdown, with sewage flowing into streams affecting Palestinian villages nearby. Few pollution indictments result in action. Israel built four plants for Palestinian waste in the 1970s, of which only one was functioning in 2007, with Israeli budgetary problems being cited for the lack of adequate infrastructure leaving most Palestinian sewage untreated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHareuveni200919–21-523] The landfill near [Al-Jiftlik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Jiftlik) in the [Jericho Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jericho_Governorate), built on [absentee Palestinian property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli_land_and_property_laws) without planning or an environmental impact analysis, is for the exclusive use 1,000 tons per day of waste produced by Israeli settlements and cities within Israel.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW2016-184] Palestinians are restricted to three landfills, and permits for more have been denied unless the sites can be used to dump settlement garbage. Even if a permit is given without this agreement, settler waste is still dumped there under military escort.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW2016-184]

### Resource extraction

According to the [Hague Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hague_Conventions_of_1899_and_1907) (Article 55) an occupying power may reap some value from the resources of the country occupied but not deplete its assets: the [usufruct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usufruct) must benefit the people under occupation. The Oslo Accords agreed to the transfer of mining rights to the Palestinian Authority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW2016-184]

Israel has given concessions for 11 settlement quarries to operate. The [World Bank](36752.md) estimates that 275 quarries could be opened in Area C, and that Israeli restrictions cost the Palestinian economy US\$241 million per year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiksicEddinCali201458-524] Palestinians are also denied permits to process Dead Sea minerals, such as [bromine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bromine), about 75% of world production of which comes from this area,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiksicEddinCali201458-524] while Israeli firms such as [Ahava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahava) do so and export to the EU. The latter restrictions are estimated to cost the Palestinian economy \$642 million.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiksicEddinCali201458–60-525]

## Economic and social benefits and costs of the occupation

Many Israeli businesses operate in the West Bank, often run by settlers who enjoy the benefits of government subsidies, low rents, favourable tax rates and access to cheap Palestinian labour. [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) claims that the "physical imprint", with 20 Israeli industrial zones covering by 2016 some 1,365 hectares, of such commercial operations, agricultural and otherwise, is more extensive than that of the settlements themselves. The restrictions on Palestinian enterprise in Area C cause unemployment which is then mopped up by industrial parks that can draw on a pool of people without job prospects if not in settlements. Some Palestinian workers at the [Barkan Industrial Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barkan_Industrial_Park) have complained anonymously that they were paid less than the minimum Israeli wage per hour (\$5.75), with payments ranging from \$1.50 to 2–4 dollars, with shifts of up to 12 hours, no vacations, sick days, pay slips or social benefits.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW2016-184] Many such businesses export abroad, which, according to [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), makes the world complicit in the settlement project.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHRW2016-184]

Israeli policy aimed to impede any form of Palestinian competition with Israeli economic interests. The approach was set forth by Israel's then Defense Minister [Yitzhak Rabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Rabin) in 1986, who stated:

> "there will be no development initiated by the Israeli Government, and no permits will be given for expanding agriculture or industry, which may compete with the State of Israel".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIsaacKhalilRishmawiDabouqi20154-526]

The [World Bank](36752.md) estimated that the annual economic costs to the Palestinian economy of the Israeli occupation of Area C alone in 2015 was 23% of GNP in direct costs, and 12% in indirect costs, totally 35% which, together with fiscal loss of revenue at 800 million dollars, totals an estimated 5.2 billion dollars.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnctad201614-527] Fiscally, one estimate places the "leakage" of Palestinian revenue back to the Israeli treasury at 17% of total Palestinian public revenue, 3.6% of GNP.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuigley20183-528] A 2015 estimate put annual Israeli government expenditure on settlements at \$US1.1 billion, though this is an inference given that the government does not report its settlement outlays.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnthonyEgalRiesBond201514–15-529] By 1982 subsidized Israeli agricultural productions and unhampered flow of Israeli manufactures hindered the growth of manufacturing industries in the Palestinian territories. High tariffs imposed by Israel on imports from countries outside the area of Israel meant Palestinian consumers had a choice of paying high prices for imported goods from foreign countries, or purchasing them from high-cost Israeli suppliers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDe_Waart1994171-530][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Arkadie1977111–112-531] Palestinian goods exporting to Israel were hit by tariffs, which down to 1991 earned Israel annually \$1,000,000, but Israeli exports to the Palestinian territories were exempted from import duties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZiai2013137-532] Since internal economic growth is hampered by Israeli restrictions, and, to compensate, 40% of the Palestinian economy relies on international aid, it is argued that such aid constitutes a subsidy to the occupation itself, making it "one of the cheapest occupations", for Israel.[^cite_note-533] The [Paris Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protocol_on_Economic_Relations) undersigned in 1994 allowed Israel to collect [VAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VAT) on all Palestinian imports and good from that country or in transit through its ports, with the system of [clearance revenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clearing_\(finance\)) giving it effective control over roughly 75% of PA income. Israel can withhold that revenue as a punitive measure, as it did in response to the decision by the PA to adhere to the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) in 2015.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQuigley201826-534]

A 2009 World Bank study concluded that "Very few economies have faced such a comprehensive array of obstacles to investment – not just of physical impediments to movement, but also comprehensive institutional and administrative barriers to economies of scale and natural resources, along with an unclear political horizon and the inability to predictably plan movement of people and goods".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWorld_Bank20096-319]

### Overall economic costs
